DL, this could not represent a relevant problem.
page=7 block=16449856 (x=1 y=30) width=171.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Complexity and level of automation
page=7 block=110756045 (x=13 y=41) width=130.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=JIRJGO+CharisSIL-BoldItalic wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
ML techniques have been extensively studied to detect attacks. DL
page=7 block=16465703 (x=13 y=51) width=239.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
techniques are newer than ML and continue to be explored as promising
page=7 block=16465703 (x=1 y=62) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
solutions to achieve high accuracy in detecting sophisticated threats.
page=7 block=16465703 (x=1 y=72) width=245.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Offline learning methods focus on two main goals: (1) to minimize
page=7 block=16465703 (x=13 y=82) width=239.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
the complexity of the security solution and (2) to maximize its detec-
page=7 block=16465703 (x=1 y=93) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
tion performance. The complexity can be reduced by optimizing the
page=7 block=16465703 (x=1 y=103) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
algorithm/model or by minimizing the number of input variables. High
page=7 block=16465703 (x=1 y=113) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
detection performance, measured in general by accuracy and false pos-
page=7 block=16465703 (x=1 y=124) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
itive (FP) rates, can be achieved with more complex algorithms/models
page=7 block=16465703 (x=1 y=134) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
and using a high number of input variables. Therefore, these techniques
page=7 block=16465703 (x=1 y=145) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
need a trade off between accuracy and complexity. DL methods have
page=7 block=16465703 (x=1 y=155) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
higher demands of these resources than ML methods.
page=7 block=16465703 (x=1 y=165) width=190.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
As the training is an offline process that can be performed on
page=7 block=16465703 (x=13 y=176) width=239.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
the idle time of the controller or on an alternative server, the time
page=7 block=16465703 (x=1 y=186) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
overhead of this process can be ignored. However, several researchers
page=7 block=16465703 (x=1 y=197) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
have proposeddata preprocessing methodologies, whose overhead is an
page=7 block=16465703 (x=1 y=207) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
important factor. Also, the periods toupdate (offline) the models should
page=7 block=16465703 (x=1 y=217) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
be defined appropriately, according to the attack detection accuracy
page=7 block=16465703 (x=1 y=228) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
desired and the computational resources available.
page=7 block=16465703 (x=1 y=238) width=181.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Although these offline learning-based techniques are robust to de-
page=7 block=16465703 (x=13 y=248) width=239.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
tect complex and zero-day attacks, they may not adapt timely to drastic
page=7 block=16465703 (x=1 y=259) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
changes in the network. Furthermore, these methods can be easily de-
page=7 block=16465703 (x=1 y=269) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
feated by intelligent attackers. These methods demand periodic human
page=7 block=16465703 (x=1 y=280) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
supervision to update/correct them. It is important to recognize that
page=7 block=16465703 (x=1 y=290) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
offline learning strategies with appropriate update periods could reach
page=7 block=16465703 (x=1 y=300) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
enough adaptability to follow timely network dynamics. Nonetheless,
page=7 block=16465703 (x=1 y=311) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
the emerging techniques of adaptive ML [79] will potentially be a
page=7 block=16465703 (x=1 y=321) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
key enabler of autonomous threat detection capable of operating in
page=7 block=16465703 (x=1 y=331) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
ever-changing contexts.
page=7 block=16465703 (x=1 y=342) width=83.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Finally, model-based attack detectionwith offline adaptation cannot
page=7 block=16465703 (x=13 y=352) width=239.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
help to achieve the self-configuration and self-optimization properties.
page=7 block=16465703 (x=1 y=363) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
4.3. Model-based attack detection with online adaptation
page=7 block=12650746 (x=1 y=383) width=193.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=TRBPLO+CharisSIL-Italic wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
As networks are constantly evolving, detection models with online
page=7 block=16485492 (x=13 y=404) width=239.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5

dynamics, thus increasing the detection rate, minimizing the FP rate,
page=7 block=16485492 (x=1 y=425) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
and reducing the detection time.

To better understand the necessity of the detection model adap-

tation, we present the concept drift in the following. Consider ?, the

In dynamically changing and non-stationary environments, the sys-
page=7 block=16545768 (x=13 y=535) width=239.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
tem behavior can change over time. The changes in the system may
page=7 block=16545768 (x=1 y=545) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
affect the probability distribution of the input data ?(?), the prior prob-

page=7 block=16545768 (x=1 y=577) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
the prediction [80]. These variations over time of the distribution
page=7 block=16545768 (x=1 y=587) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
probabilities are known as the phenomenon of concept drift.
page=7 block=16545768 (x=1 y=597) width=213.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
Computer networks are dynamic and non-stationary environments
page=7 block=16545768 (x=13 y=608) width=239.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
where the concept drift is present. Therefore, threat detection models
page=7 block=16545768 (x=1 y=618) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
need to have mechanisms to adapt to evolving data over time. Other-
page=7 block=16545768 (x=1 y=628) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
wise, their performance will degrade. These models must be updated
page=7 block=16545768 (x=1 y=639) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
with the new data distribution or be replaced to meet the changed
page=7 block=16545768 (x=1 y=649) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
situation.
page=7 block=16545768 (x=1 y=660) width=33.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=19
Online learning strategies can be adequate to deal with the concept
page=7 block=16545768 (x=13 y=670) width=239.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
drift since they process data sequentially. These strategies can produce
page=7 block=16545768 (x=1 y=680) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
a model with a partial training dataset, and the model is continu-
page=7 block=16545768 (x=1 y=691) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
ously updated during the operation as more information arrives. Fig. 5

Fig. 5. General structure of online adaptive learning.
page=7 block=-1054080404 (x=317 y=155) width=156.000000 height=4.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
shows the general structure of online adaptive learning that can effec-
page=7 block=146412473 (x=270 y=187) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
tively prevent the concept drift, according to [80]. The online learning
page=7 block=146412473 (x=270 y=198) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
procedure has the following steps:
page=7 block=146412473 (x=270 y=208) width=122.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
(i) Predict when a new example arrives, using the current model;
page=7 block=136585423 (x=281 y=227) width=235.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
(ii) Diagnose, when receiving the correct label (delayed), the loss
page=7 block=136585423 (x=279 y=237) width=242.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
function is estimated; and
page=7 block=136585423 (x=294 y=248) width=92.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
(iii) Update the model, if required, using the new example.
page=7 block=136585423 (x=276 y=258) width=213.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
In this structure, a Memory module defines how and which data is
page=7 block=146526380 (x=282 y=276) width=239.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
presented to the Learning module. The Loss Estimation module tracksthe
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=287) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
performance of the learning algorithm. It also sends information to the
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=297) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
Change Detection module to update the model if required. For details of
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=307) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
each component in this architecture, please refer to [80].
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=318) width=204.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
From the implementation perspective, as model adaptation is on-
page=7 block=146526380 (x=282 y=328) width=239.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
line, it is necessary to consider the SDN controller processing ca-
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=338) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
pabilities. Moreover, the initial model can be trained offline, using
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=349) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
historical data, and loaded into the SDN controller. Subsequently, the
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=359) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
mechanisms of online adaptation will update the model using measures
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=370) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
gathered from the network. In general, we assume that online learning
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=380) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
mechanisms are unsupervised since there is no immediate and direct
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=390) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
feedback about the model decisions. Therefore, anomaly detection
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=401) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
mechanisms presented in the literature [81] are in the category of
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=411) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
model-based attack detection with online adaptation, as long as they
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=421) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
update the model according to network variations. In this way, unsu-
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=432) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
pervised and adaptive ML/DL methods are in the category presented in
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=442) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
this section.
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=452) width=42.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
For instance, work in [82] presents an online learning algorithm
page=7 block=146526380 (x=282 y=463) width=239.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
to detect Heavy Hitters (HH) in SDN networks. HH are entities whose
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=473) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
number of network elements is at least a specific fraction of the total
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=484) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
number of network elements (e.g., flows whose volumes are larger
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=494) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
than certain thresholds). This study detects heavy traffic. A model
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=504) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
receives flows collected from the switches. A market list ? contains
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=515) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
HHs historically identified. The online learning mechanism maps every

incoming flow to a network state (? = 0 for HH and ? = 1 for normal
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=535) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
network activity) according to its bytes and weights changes. The
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=546) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
weights and thresholds in ? are pre-defined by the administrator based
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=556) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
on the characteristics of the flow and a historical dataset. The network
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=566) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
state feedbacks on the market list ?. The OF protocol gathers statistics
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=577) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
from the DP. To reduce the overhead of gathering flow information
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=587) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
of the switches, the controller decides how to change the period of
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=598) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
collecting statistics and sends coarse-grained or fine-grained rules to
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=608) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
the switch as a zoom-out or zoom-in of existing rules. The evaluation
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=618) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
of this proposal, based on detection accuracy, detection time, and
page=7 block=146526380 (x=270 y=629) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
communication overhead, demonstrates its effectiveness.

Although we can find several studies that propose online unsu-
page=7 block=146526380 (x=282 y=649) width=239.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
pervised learning attack detection (anomaly detection), the incursion

on developing comprehensive online learning mechanisms to detect

attacks on networks leveraging the SDN technology is limited. A com-

plete online detection method should solve the concept drift, explained

before, using a structure similar to that shown in Fig. 5. The limited

71

exploration in this due to online learning does not necessarily mean su-

Streaming algorithms are another approach to achieve adaptability
page=8 block=16452896 (x=13 y=71) width=239.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5

methods. They can process high-speed continuous flows and use limited
page=8 block=16452896 (x=1 y=91) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
processing and memory. Therefore, examples are sequentially pro-

cessed as they arrive in a few steps. Streaming algorithms are less

by batch to update the decision model and offer faster adaptation than
page=8 block=16452896 (x=1 y=132) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
offline learning. Streaming algorithms can have random access to pre-
page=8 block=16452896 (x=1 y=142) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
vious examples or representative/selected examples. Thus, streaming
page=8 block=16452896 (x=1 y=152) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
algorithms can effectively detect threats in SDN, offering a high level
page=8 block=16452896 (x=1 y=163) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
of adaptability to network changes and the scalability required since
page=8 block=16452896 (x=1 y=173) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
resources on the controller are limited.

Complexity and level of automation

Model-based attack detection techniques with an online adapta-

tion that solves the concept drift require further explorations. These

vantage of online learning is the low requirement of storage capac-

model adaptation. The implementation of these techniques may require

elevated expertise in implementing sophisticated algorithms.

In addition, these techniques learn the network behavior in real-
page=8 block=16477550 (x=13 y=275) width=239.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
time. Therefore, they provide the advanced capabilities needed to adapt

against zero-day attacks. These conditions make these techniques to be

less dependent on human supervision. However, AI-enabled attacks can

still represent a big concern for these methods. Finally, online adapta-

tion only on the attack detection cannot enable the self-configuration
page=8 block=16477550 (x=1 y=325) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
and self-optimization properties.
page=8 block=16477550 (x=1 y=336) width=115.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
4.4. Reinforcement learning for threat mitigation
page=8 block=10750034 (x=1 y=355) width=164.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=TRBPLO+CharisSIL-Italic wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Reactive solutions require a mitigation part after an attack has been
page=8 block=16574036 (x=13 y=375) width=239.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
detected. Blocking or dropping malicious traffic is the most basic miti-
page=8 block=16574036 (x=1 y=386) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
gation strategy [56,59,63,67,83–85], and it assumes an attack detection
page=8 block=16574036 (x=1 y=396) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
performance of 100%. However, even complex DL-based solutions
page=8 block=16574036 (x=1 y=406) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
cannot achieve such perfection in detecting attacks. Moreover, other
page=8 block=16574036 (x=1 y=416) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
mitigation strategies have been proposed, such as delaying suspicious
page=8 block=16574036 (x=1 y=426) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
traffic [86–88] or redirecting the suspicious traffic to Network Pro-
page=8 block=16574036 (x=1 y=437) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
cessor Units (NPUs) such as deep packet inspection (DPI) modules
page=8 block=16574036 (x=1 y=447) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
for deeper traffic analysis [62,89]. These approaches are intended
page=8 block=16574036 (x=1 y=457) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
to reduce the effect of legitimate traffic affected by false positives.
page=8 block=16574036 (x=1 y=467) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
However, in these solutions, attackers still use part of the network
page=8 block=16574036 (x=1 y=477) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
resources.
page=8 block=16574036 (x=1 y=487) width=35.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=19
A need arose for a defense system that would not only detect an
page=8 block=16574036 (x=13 y=498) width=239.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
intrusion on time but also would make the most optimal real-time
page=8 block=16574036 (x=1 y=508) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
crisis-action decision on how the network security policy should be
page=8 block=16574036 (x=1 y=518) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
modified to mitigate the threat. Thus, mitigation solutions based on
page=8 block=16574036 (x=1 y=528) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
Reinforcement Learning (RL) have been proposed.
page=8 block=16574036 (x=1 y=538) width=179.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
In the RL paradigm, software agents automatically define the ideal
page=8 block=16574036 (x=13 y=548) width=239.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
behavior within a specific context by continually making value judg-
page=8 block=16574036 (x=1 y=559) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
ments to select good actions over bad [90]. In a typical RL model, an
page=8 block=16574036 (x=1 y=569) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
agent monitors the environment via perception. It also takes actions
page=8 block=16574036 (x=1 y=579) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
within the environment, via an actuator, which could change the
page=8 block=16574036 (x=1 y=589) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
state of the environment. At each time step, the agent receives an
page=8 block=16574036 (x=1 y=599) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
observational input reflecting the state ? of the environment. In turn,
page=8 block=16574036 (x=1 y=610) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
it can take an action ? that transfers the state of the network into a
page=8 block=16574036 (x=1 y=620) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
new state ??. This is conveyed to the agent, along with a reinforcement
page=8 block=16574036 (x=1 y=629) width=251.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
signal ?, indicating the value and desirability of the action held [91].
page=8 block=16574036 (x=1 y=640) width=247.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Attack mitigation proposals configure certain states, action-sets,
page=8 block=16574036 (x=13 y=650) width=239.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
and rewards to implement the RL to automate the attack mitigation
page=8 block=16574036 (x=1 y=660) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
[90,92–94]. Work in [91] proposed a unified general scheme for de-
page=8 block=16574036 (x=1 y=671) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
signing complex and adaptive RL agents for the network threat mitiga-
page=8 block=16574036 (x=1 y=681) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
tion task. This proposal is able to automate the mitigation of complex
page=8 block=16574036 (x=1 y=691) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
attacks, such as APT.
page=8 block=16574036 (x=1 y=701) width=75.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
RL-based mitigation strategies still face critical practical obstacles.
page=8 block=146408164 (x=282 y=20) width=239.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Low sample efficiency and reward function specification are possi-
page=8 block=146408164 (x=270 y=31) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
bly the biggest obstacles to applying RL algorithms. Other critical
page=8 block=146408164 (x=270 y=41) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
shortcomings of RL are:
page=8 block=146408164 (x=270 y=52) width=84.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
• It is a very immature technique;
page=8 block=119150092 (x=283 y=68) width=121.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
• It is a highly computational extensive technique and data-hungry,
page=8 block=119150092 (x=283 y=79) width=238.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
that is, it requires to interact with the environment at each new
page=8 block=119150092 (x=290 y=92) width=231.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
training iteration;
page=8 block=119150092 (x=290 y=102) width=63.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
• It requires stationary or fully simulated environments;
page=8 block=119150092 (x=283 y=111) width=200.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
• It requires deep expertise in its implementation; and
page=8 block=119150092 (x=283 y=122) width=194.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
• Most current data science and ML platforms do not have RL
page=8 block=119150092 (x=283 y=133) width=238.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
capabilities.
page=8 block=119150092 (x=290 y=145) width=42.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=19
RL promises an active and automated solution to complex threats.
page=8 block=146410048 (x=282 y=164) width=239.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
However, further exploration is needed.
page=8 block=146410048 (x=270 y=174) width=142.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
Complexity and level of automation
page=8 block=-1892416611 (x=282 y=185) width=130.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=JIRJGO+CharisSIL-BoldItalic wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
RL-based mitigation strategies are adaptive to the environment
page=8 block=146429069 (x=282 y=195) width=239.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
and changes in the attacks. However, these methods are emerging,
page=8 block=146429069 (x=270 y=206) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
and crucial practical challenges need to be solved, such as elevated
page=8 block=146429069 (x=270 y=216) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
computation and data storage demands. Moreover, it requires a high
page=8 block=146429069 (x=270 y=227) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
level of expertise to define the states’ models and reward function
page=8 block=146429069 (x=270 y=237) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
shaping. Nevertheless, solving these issues will lead to a big step
page=8 block=146429069 (x=270 y=248) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
towards security automation in networks.
page=8 block=146429069 (x=270 y=258) width=148.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
RL-mitigation methods combined with Network Function Virtual-
page=8 block=146429069 (x=282 y=269) width=239.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
ization technology could provide the capabilities of self-configuration
page=8 block=146429069 (x=270 y=279) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
and self-optimization to protect networks. However, further efforts are
page=8 block=146429069 (x=270 y=290) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
required to solve several related issues, such as the scalability property.
page=8 block=146429069 (x=270 y=300) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
4.5. Adversarial learning
page=8 block=1486358412 (x=270 y=322) width=84.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=TRBPLO+CharisSIL-Italic wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
All defense proposals studied previously unrealistically assume that
page=8 block=146532276 (x=282 y=343) width=239.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
the attackers are not aware of the defense mechanisms. However, once
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=354) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
deployed these implementations in production networks, intelligent
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=364) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
and adaptive adversaries may violate the initial assumption of the AI
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=375) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
techniques. The commoditization of ML tools can enable attackers to
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=386) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
perform many experiments to identify how to use them for malicious
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=396) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
purposes. Also, the use of public datasets on security model training
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=407) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
is a problem because attackers could use the information to create a
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=417) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
strategy for poisoning training data, known as adversarial samples.
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=428) width=239.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Most ML methods are sensitive to adversarial samples, e.g., regres-
page=8 block=146532276 (x=282 y=438) width=239.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
sion models, SVM, DT, RF, NN, and KNN. In addition, work in [95]
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=449) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
demonstrated that adversarial attacks have a relatively high impact on
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=459) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
different DL-based detections, such as (MLP), CNN, and LSTM, resulting
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=470) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
in a reduction of the detection accuracy and increasing the training
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=480) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
time. Even adaptive techniques, such as RL, can be affected by Adver-
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=491) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
sarial Machine Learning (AML) methods. Authors in [96] demonstrated
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=501) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
two ways of how AML can affect RL-based defense performance: (1) the
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=512) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
AML applies a perturbation to the state observed by the target so that
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=522) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
the agent chooses a different action as the optimal, and (2) the AML
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=533) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
corrupts the reward function, so the direction towards the loss function
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=543) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
minimization is perturbed, which results in delayed optimal policy im-
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=554) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
plementation. The study showed that even these simple attacks might
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=564) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
cause RL agents to take suboptimal actions. The authors suggested that
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=575) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
simple solution ideas such as adding a 5% error to the reward signal in
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=585) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
free attack periods can help the agent learn optimal actions, although
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=596) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
some delay is maintained.
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=606) width=92.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
The defense methods must be in constant work against adversarial
page=8 block=146532276 (x=282 y=617) width=239.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
activities. The work in [97] proposed to reduce the impact of adversar-
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=627) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
ial impact by generating synthetic attacks that can target networks with
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=638) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
SDN architecture and using them to train a detection model to capture
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=649) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
different attack variations. They proposed a Generative Adversarial
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=659) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
Deep Neural Network (GAN) to automate the generation of realistic
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=670) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
data in a semi-supervised manner. GAN applies a set of non-linear trans-
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=680) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
formations on an original malicious sample to generate an adversarial
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=691) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
example that evades classification models. GAN helped the adversarial
page=8 block=146532276 (x=270 y=701) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Page: 9 left:1 upper:1
N.M. Yungaicela-Naula, C. Vargas-Rosales, J.A. Pérez-Díaz et al.
page=9 block=11796484 (x=1 y=1) width=180.000000 height=4.000000 fontname=TRBPLO+CharisSIL-Italic wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Computer Communications 183 (2022) 64–82
page=9 block=-998244348 (x=393 y=1) width=128.000000 height=4.000000 fontname=TRBPLO+CharisSIL-Italic wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
72
page=9 block=117902879 (x=257 y=723) width=7.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=15
examples generation that can deceive two ML detection models for
page=9 block=16451536 (x=1 y=20) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
SDN, KNN, and RF classification models. The authors evaluated the
page=9 block=16451536 (x=1 y=31) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
decrease in attack detection rate as the effect of adversarial attacks.
page=9 block=16451536 (x=1 y=41) width=242.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Protecting AI-powered security systems and considering malicious
page=9 block=16451536 (x=13 y=51) width=239.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
use of AI by attackersare part of the security strategy technology trends
page=9 block=16451536 (x=1 y=62) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
for 2020 [98]. Adversarial learning jointly with SDN technology has
page=9 block=16451536 (x=1 y=72) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
not been widely explored to solve the security problem in networks.
page=9 block=16451536 (x=1 y=83) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
As the attacks are becoming complex, the use of methods capable of
page=9 block=16451536 (x=1 y=93) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
defeating intelligent adversary attacks is becoming critical towards the
page=9 block=16451536 (x=1 y=104) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
automation of network defense.
page=9 block=16451536 (x=1 y=114) width=113.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Complexity and level of automation
page=9 block=110756465 (x=13 y=125) width=130.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=JIRJGO+CharisSIL-BoldItalic wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Methods that countermeasure adversarial AI-enabled attacks require
page=9 block=16465871 (x=13 y=135) width=239.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
high computation capacity, medium storage extent, and elevated AI
page=9 block=16465871 (x=1 y=146) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
implementation abilities. These methods can timely adapt to zero-day
page=9 block=16465871 (x=1 y=156) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
attacks and thwart AI-enabled adversaries. That makes these methods
page=9 block=16465871 (x=1 y=167) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
highly independent of human supervision.
page=9 block=16465871 (x=1 y=177) width=150.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
These methods can be enhanced using the Network Function Vir-
page=9 block=16465871 (x=13 y=188) width=239.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
tualization technology to provide the capabilities of self-configuration
page=9 block=16465871 (x=1 y=198) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
and self-optimization. Nevertheless, these methods have not
page=9 block=16465871 (x=1 y=209) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=11
been widely explored. Sophisticated and smart attacks are expected to
page=9 block=16465871 (x=1 y=219) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
target networks in the following years. Therefore, it is recommended
page=9 block=16465871 (x=1 y=230) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
to keep exploring adversarial learning techniques that enable the
page=9 block=16465871 (x=1 y=240) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
detection of dynamic threats in adversarial environments.
page=9 block=16465871 (x=1 y=250) width=206.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
5. SDN-based proactive defense
page=9 block=7931482 (x=1 y=271) width=121.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=TWLFEH+CharisSIL-Bold wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Reactive approaches imply obtaining as much telemetry as possible
page=9 block=16500225 (x=13 y=293) width=239.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
to detect potential attacks. Even if SDN simplifies and centralizes the
page=9 block=16500225 (x=1 y=303) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
network monitoring and control, the detection/mitigation approach
page=9 block=16500225 (x=1 y=313) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
is costly for the defender since logs management is storage greedy,
page=9 block=16500225 (x=1 y=324) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
and threat detection is expensive in computation. Furthermore, the
page=9 block=16500225 (x=1 y=334) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
attackers will briefly affect the network performance until they are
page=9 block=16500225 (x=1 y=345) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
detected, causing considerable costs. The defender’s role is unfair in
page=9 block=16500225 (x=1 y=355) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
this approach since it attempts to prevent intrusions at every possible
page=9 block=16500225 (x=1 y=366) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
location. Meanwhile, the attacker only needs to discover and exploit a
page=9 block=16500225 (x=1 y=376) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
single vulnerability to breach the complete defense.
page=9 block=16500225 (x=1 y=387) width=184.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
In contrast, proactive defense methods allow inverting this asymme-
page=9 block=16500225 (x=13 y=397) width=239.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
try between attackers and defenders. These methods implement mech-
page=9 block=16500225 (x=1 y=408) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
anisms to evade/prevent attacks or reduce the attack surfaces in the
page=9 block=16500225 (x=1 y=418) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
network. SDN has beenafundamental element in designingand deploy-
page=9 block=16500225 (x=1 y=429) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
ing proactivedefense for networks, given its centralized monitoring and
page=9 block=16500225 (x=1 y=439) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
control of networks with dynamic programming capability.
page=9 block=16500225 (x=1 y=450) width=211.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
Next, existing methods of proactive defenses are analyzed.
page=9 block=16500225 (x=13 y=460) width=208.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
5.1. Moving target defense (MTD)
page=9 block=7605062 (x=1 y=481) width=116.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=TRBPLO+CharisSIL-Italic wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
MTD consists of mechanisms that automatically implement diverse
page=9 block=16552446 (x=13 y=502) width=239.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
and dynamic network configurations to make the system attack surface
page=9 block=16552446 (x=1 y=513) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
unpredictable to adversaries. As a result, the system status with the
page=9 block=16552446 (x=1 y=523) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
MTD strategy is hard to exploit and more resilient to different forms of
page=9 block=16552446 (x=1 y=534) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
attacks [99,100].
page=9 block=16552446 (x=1 y=544) width=61.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
MTD mechanisms are not new as they have been used in conven-
page=9 block=16552446 (x=13 y=555) width=239.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
tional networks. However, in these traditional architectures, the im-
page=9 block=16552446 (x=1 y=565) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
plementation of MTD requires the reconfiguration and synchronization
page=9 block=16552446 (x=1 y=575) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
of network devices, interrupting the current communication sessions,
page=9 block=16552446 (x=1 y=586) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
evidencing low efficiency and high cost. Using SDN architecture that
page=9 block=16552446 (x=1 y=596) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
offers a highly flexible and dynamic programmable network, we can
page=9 block=16552446 (x=1 y=607) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
deploy specialized network applications and services to create robust
page=9 block=16552446 (x=1 y=617) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
MTD systems.
page=9 block=16552446 (x=1 y=628) width=49.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
The adaptive modification ofIP addresses and ports of hostscan pre-
page=9 block=16552446 (x=13 y=638) width=239.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
vent recognizing-based attacks like man-in-the-middle and port scan-
page=9 block=16552446 (x=1 y=649) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
ning attacks [101]. We canrandomly replace the sourceanddestination
page=9 block=16552446 (x=1 y=659) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
IP address of packets with virtual IP addresses in the SDN switches,
page=9 block=16552446 (x=1 y=670) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
which enhances the unpredictability of the network behavior [102].
page=9 block=16552446 (x=1 y=680) width=243.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
The selection of dynamic routes provides significant advantages to
page=9 block=16552446 (x=13 y=691) width=239.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
avoid adversaries eavesdrop on the network links or launch DDoS/DoS
page=9 block=16552446 (x=1 y=701) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
attacks on certain network links. However, they are still predictable
page=9 block=146411524 (x=270 y=20) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
and cannot provide resilience against intelligent adversaries. The use
page=9 block=146411524 (x=270 y=31) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
of random routing mutation (RRM) techniques can effectively defend
page=9 block=146411524 (x=270 y=41) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
the network while satisfying the performance and QoS requirements of
page=9 block=146411524 (x=270 y=51) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
the network [103]. Also, data of various users can be mixed, among
page=9 block=146411524 (x=270 y=62) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
different routes, making it difficult for attackers to recover complete
page=9 block=146411524 (x=270 y=72) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
data from one communication and avoiding attackers to separate data
page=9 block=146411524 (x=270 y=83) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
for a single user [104].
page=9 block=146411524 (x=270 y=93) width=82.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
The lack of dynamism on the mutation cycles of RRM methods
page=9 block=146411524 (x=282 y=104) width=239.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
and their difficulty to search for an optimal mutation routing path
page=9 block=146411524 (x=270 y=114) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
represents a weakness against advanced adversarial. To deal with these
page=9 block=146411524 (x=270 y=125) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
problems, the generation of the routing paths can be specified and
page=9 block=146411524 (x=270 y=135) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
solved as an optimization problem, considering the constraints of rout-
page=9 block=146411524 (x=270 y=146) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
ing paths’ randomness and network load balance [105]. SDN facilitates
page=9 block=146411524 (x=270 y=156) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
this approach, given its global network monitoring and in-line network
page=9 block=146411524 (x=270 y=167) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
configuration.
page=9 block=146411524 (x=270 y=177) width=49.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=19
The limitation of the MTD mechanisms is the scalability since they
page=9 block=146411524 (x=282 y=188) width=239.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
require monitoring the network state in real-time, which means a high
page=9 block=146411524 (x=270 y=198) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
burden on the controller and a high charge on the control channel to
page=9 block=146411524 (x=270 y=209) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
gather the statistics from the switches.
page=9 block=146411524 (x=270 y=219) width=137.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Complexity and level of automation
page=9 block=-1892416386 (x=282 y=230) width=130.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=JIRJGO+CharisSIL-BoldItalic wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
From the proactive security approaches, MTD solutions have been
page=9 block=146426384 (x=282 y=240) width=239.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
more studied. Nevertheless, further efforts are required to obtain scal-
page=9 block=146426384 (x=270 y=250) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
able solutions. These mechanisms demand high capacities of com-
page=9 block=146426384 (x=270 y=261) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
putational processing and storage to maintain their dynamism. As
page=9 block=146426384 (x=270 y=271) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
a result, it provides adaptability against zero-day and intelligent at-
page=9 block=146426384 (x=270 y=282) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
tacks. The combination of MTD with Network Function Virtualization
page=9 block=146426384 (x=270 y=292) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
(NFV) technology enables the Self-configuration and self-optimization
page=9 block=146426384 (x=270 y=303) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
capabilities.
page=9 block=146426384 (x=270 y=313) width=42.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=19
5.2. Network function virtualization (NFV)
page=9 block=-1711536172 (x=270 y=334) width=146.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=TRBPLO+CharisSIL-Italic wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
NFV is a network architecture paradigm that virtualizes different
page=9 block=146532384 (x=282 y=355) width=239.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
network node functions, making these network functions (NFs) and
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=366) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
services much more adaptive and scalable. NFV can significantly re-
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=376) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
duce the hardware cost, greatly improve operation efficiency, and
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=387) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
dramatically shorten the development of the lifecycle of a network
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=397) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
service [106].
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=408) width=49.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Although SDN and NFV are two distinct and independent technolo-
page=9 block=146532384 (x=282 y=418) width=239.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
gies, combining them in a network can bring multiple advantages to
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=429) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
emerging technologies. SDN provides centralized knowledge about the
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=439) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
network status and programmability, whereas NFV boosts the develop-
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=450) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
ment of virtualized network appliances executed on top of commodity
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=460) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
servers [107].
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=471) width=49.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Next, we review new strategies to defend networks using SDN and
page=9 block=146532384 (x=282 y=481) width=239.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
NFV. Work in [108] presents a proactive defense against complex
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=492) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
DDoS attacks. This approach uses virtual networks (VN) to dynamically
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=502) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
reallocate the network resources without disrupting the network service
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=513) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
or violating the VN properties. The components of this solution are
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=523) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
(1) the correct-by-construction VN migration planning and (2) the VN
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=534) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
migration mechanism. The former implements and solves a constraint
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=544) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
satisfaction problem using Satisfiability Modulo Theory (SMT). The
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=554) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
second component controls the execution of the VN migration strat-
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=565) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
egy on a virtualized physical network. This component handles all
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=575) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
movement logistics, including the precise sequencing/scheduling of the
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=586) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
steps’ realization to complete the move and the timing of such process.
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=596) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
The solution was tested against a crossfire attack. It showed high
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=607) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
effectiveness in restoring the downgraded bandwidth because of the
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=617) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
DDoS attack through the timely migration to a safety threat placement.
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=628) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
Work in [109] proposes a defense system for IoT networks based on
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=638) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
SDN and NFV. A reinforcement learning agent is used to evaluate the
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=649) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
risks of potential attacks and take optimal actions to mitigate them.
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=659) width=242.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
SHIELD [110] was a project funded by the European Union to
page=9 block=146532384 (x=282 y=670) width=239.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
develop a security framework that offers Security-as-a-Service (SaS).
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=680) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
SHIELD applied NFV/SDN for virtualization and dynamic placement of
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=691) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
virtualized security functions in the network. In combination with Big
page=9 block=146532384 (x=270 y=701) width=251.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=CZEFFH+CharisSIL wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5

N.M. Yungaicela-Naula, C. Vargas-Rosales, J.A. Pérez-Díaz et al.

Computer Communications 183 (2022) 64–82

73
